The daily field guide for digital media

AI Media Scout

2026-11-19 · All platforms · Open-source software

This edition provides 76 complete documentation guides and 6 additional screened leads. 16 earlier screened entries received full guides. New and updated records carry their own dated evidence checks. This edition looks at archival film and recorded-audio work, camera-driven projection, editable digital publishing, and handoffs for motion, captions and creative production. Software licensing is reviewed independently of model terms, any paid service or required proprietary host. Documentation and published examples informed the quality checks. No discovered app was installed or run. The frozen plan covered 112 fields with 1264/1264 repository queries attempted. 1236 completed successfully, 2 were partial, and 26 failed. 233 were bounded. All 31 configured model tasks were attempted. 0 model tasks failed. The collection retained explicit warnings and limits. Metadata screening indexed all 2,601 saved repository pages, spanning 80,581 numeric identities. The complete retained backlog was consulted alongside today's metadata, including earlier pilot candidates. It currently has 115,508 raw records and 1,142 previously screened pending guides. A metadata signal does not establish software eligibility. Unreviewed and excluded candidates remain in private research records. Live primary research beyond GitHub covered all observed fields and the configured ecosystems. The notes identify limits such as access and rate restrictions, failed fetches and bounded discovery pages. The counts represent the documented searches and available pages. Evidence label counts were 0 Recommended, 1 Ready to try, 76 Quality unverified, 5 Experimental. Beat This! meets the Ready to try criteria for the stated scope. Beat This! 1.1.0, default final0 model, offline beat and downbeat markers from audio using a local CPU-supported PyTorch environment. Credential-pattern material in unsealed source captures was redacted before sealing. The original and redacted content hashes and provenance stay in the local evidence record. 30 model tasks reached the configured maximum of 50 returned rows each. The model listings are bounded discovery leads. Model-task responses with no rows: text2text-generation. The separate watchlist and evidence enrichment recorded 62 individual watchlist failures and 4 evidence failures. Source failures are retained as gaps.

Search and review counts

112 creative fields; 1264/1264 repository queries attempted; 31/31 model-task queries attempted. 81352 distinct source candidates and 1377 model leads. 76 detailed profiles and 6 additional screened discoveries. Source gaps: 26 failed repository queries, 2 partial repository queries, 233 bounded repository queries, 0 failed model-task queries and 0 web ecosystem gaps. Raw search candidates include duplicates of known tools, excluded projects and projects awaiting review; they are not verified recommendations.

Profiles are based on current primary documentation. Creative quality and performance claims are attributed to the developers unless hands-on testing is explicitly recorded. Unknown hardware requirements stay unknown. Code, model weights, hosted services, and required proprietary hosts can have different terms.

Proximity Light

This is the first complete guide in the library.

Scope: A single-wall installation where a visitor approaching the camera changes the projected color or casts a moving dark region.

Evidence checked 2026-11-19; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code. Models, services and assets have separate terms. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The project supplies a synthetic three-panel frame showing camera input, estimated depth and colored projection. It does not demonstrate alignment, delay or reliability on a live installation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Serve the source folder locally, then open its localhost URL in Chrome or Edge. The project documents a PowerShell server as well as a standard Python HTTP server route. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: The current repository publishes the source and instructions. The checked releases endpoint showed no releases, and this review did not establish a maintained deployment or support policy.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: The reviewed browser source names Transformers.js, ONNX Community Depth Anything V2 and a separately downloaded MediaPipe Pose Landmarker. Upstream Depth Anything V2 Small is Apache-2.0. The upstream Base and Large models use CC BY-NC 4.0. Verify the exact ONNX conversion and pose artifact terms for the chosen route. Local app and compute. Model and browser code are fetched from external hosts at first use. No subscription is documented for the default route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

This assessment is based on published sources. Hands-on verification remains pending. Relative monocular depth can shift between frames. The image warp aligns one plane and differs on objects at other distances. Calibration, camera permission and initial CDN/model availability are setup dependencies.

How it uses AI

Depth Anything V2 predicts relative distance from an ordinary webcam. A separate MediaPipe pose mode follows people with a soft spotlight. The browser shapes and warps each result for a second display. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

This small installation tool gives a webcam and projector separate capture and output controls in an ordinary browser window. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

A single-wall installation where a visitor approaching the camera changes the projected color or casts a moving dark region. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The project supplies a synthetic three-panel frame showing camera input, estimated depth and colored projection. It does not demonstrate alignment, delay or reliability on a live installation. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

Serve the source folder locally, then open its localhost URL in Chrome or Edge. The project documents a PowerShell server as well as a standard Python HTTP server route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Serve the source folder locally, then open its localhost URL in Chrome or Edge. The project documents a PowerShell server as well as a standard Python HTTP server route.
python -m http.server

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Start the camera and select the small depth model. Open the projector window on an extended display.
  2. Align the camera crop and corner-pin the projection against a flat surface. Freeze a reference background before introducing a visitor.
  3. Try the pose-following mode separately. Inspect lag, light spill and missed people under representative lighting.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Hardware
Webcam, projector or second display, and a WebGPU-capable setup for the accelerated path. The guide names approximately 50 MB, 195 MB and 669 MB model downloads. Minimum system RAM and VRAM are undocumented.
Software
Recent Chrome or Edge with WebGPU, plus a local HTTP server. WASM is documented as a slower fallback. The core app is HTML and JavaScript.
Platforms
Browser deployment in Chrome or Edge. Windows-specific PowerShell and extended-desktop instructions are documented. Compatibility for each GPU and OS has not been established.

  • Weights: The reviewed browser source names Transformers.js, ONNX Community Depth Anything V2 and a separately downloaded MediaPipe Pose Landmarker. Upstream Depth Anything V2 Small is Apache-2.0. The upstream Base and Large models use CC BY-NC 4.0. Verify the exact ONNX conversion and pose artifact terms for the chosen route.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. The reviewed browser source names Transformers.js, ONNX Community Depth Anything V2 and a separately downloaded MediaPipe Pose Landmarker. Upstream Depth Anything V2 Small is Apache-2.0. The upstream Base and Large models use CC BY-NC 4.0. Verify the exact ONNX conversion and pose artifact terms for the chosen route.
  • Cost: Local app and compute. Model and browser code are fetched from external hosts at first use. No subscription is documented for the default route.

How it uses AI

A documented ONNX process runs compatible learned detection, pose or depth models on a camera or video texture. Score can turn the results into timeline controls for live video, sound and lighting. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Introduction

The intermedia sequencer puts video, sound, lights and device inputs on one show timeline. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

What it is good for

A camera-triggered interactive media timeline, such as mapping one face landmark to the position of a localized projected effect. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Demo & examples

The author publishes face-landmark radial-blur and camera-zone examples. They document wiring and sample project output. Venue performance and stability depend on the exact build, model and equipment. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Install

Use the official desktop installation for the target system, confirm that it includes the ONNX processes, and load a model compatible with the chosen detector. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the official desktop installation for the target system, confirm that it includes the ONNX processes, and load a model compatible with the chosen detector.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  1. Add a camera or video device into the project and route its texture to an ONNX detection or pose process.
  2. Inspect the model output and extract the relevant coordinate. Map it to one effect or zone before creating a complete timeline.
  3. Set up the output window separately. Validate model latency, output mapping and the behavior when a detection is lost.

ossia score plus ONNX Runtime support in the build. Model compatibility and the available CPU or GPU execution provider depend on the packaging and selected process.
Platforms
The installation guide lists Windows, macOS, Linux including ARM, and FreeBSD. The scoped camera and ONNX route additionally depends on build options and drivers. Web builds have distinct limitations.

How it uses AI

The project creates damage masks and paired clean/degraded clips for training film-restoration models. The separate real-film benchmark supports comparison of learned restoration systems. The damage generator itself is a scripted augmentation pipeline. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

This technical package produces controlled grayscale comparisons for research into damaged early film. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Preparing controlled grayscale training examples and comparing dust and scratch removal across short early-film clips. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The paper and README show clean input, generated grayscale damage and a defect mask. They also report a blinded preference study. The study evaluates the authors’ experiment and does not prove recovery of original image content. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

The project documents a locked uv environment, Python 3.11–3.13, and a separate step for the blue-noise basis and film textures. Read the dataset and texture terms before downloading inputs. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. The project documents a locked uv environment, Python 3.11–3.13, and a separate step for the blue-noise basis and film textures. Read the dataset and texture terms before downloading inputs.
uv sync
uv run python scripts/degrade.py input_path=data/REDS/val_sharp output_path=data/out

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Keep a clean reference and put clips in the documented folder-per-clip format. Start with a handful of lossless frames.
  2. Generate an altered copy plus a soft spatial-defect mask using a fixed seed.
  3. Inspect the masks and frame-to-frame artifacts before using them for training. Keep the held-out real film evaluation apart from synthetic pairs.

Platforms
The source instructions include a Windows virtual-environment activation example and shell instructions for Unix-like platforms. A full platform support matrix is not documented.

This assessment is based on published sources. Hands-on verification remains pending. Speech enhancement can alter transients, music and archival timbre. Alignment and preservation of room sound require listening. Release installer availability does not establish each advertised hardware combination.

How it uses AI

The documented auto_pure audio path uses BS-Roformer to split tracks and UVR-DeNoise for neural cleanup, then aligns processed audio to the original video and remuxes a new file. [1] [3]

Introduction

The project provides a specialized cleanup and remuxing route for the sound already present on a video transfer. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Making a listening copy of noisy return audio from a digitized home video while retaining original picture and time reference. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The project documents its four phases and publishes hardware-validation fixtures. Compare original and restored passages by ear, especially where music overlaps speech. The research run did not reproduce the project’s examples. [1] [3]

Install

Use the release package appropriate to the OS, or the documented Python and FFmpeg environment. Inspect the selected model configuration and hardware route before a full tape. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the release package appropriate to the OS, or the documented Python and FFmpeg environment. Inspect the selected model configuration and hardware route before a full tape.
python restore_audio_hybrid.py input.mp4

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Work from a copy of a short transfer with both speech and room ambience. Run the auto_pure mode first.
  2. Compare audio alignment at the start and end before exporting longer material.
  3. Compare both stems and the final mix against the original. Record settings with the derivative.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
An appropriate CPU or supported accelerator for the chosen model route, and disk for source and derivatives. The readme discusses VRAM-based scheduling. A universal minimum RAM, VRAM and disk requirement is undocumented.
Software
Python 3.12, Poetry for the source route, and FFmpeg. The documentation distinguishes NVIDIA CUDA, Apple MPS and CPU choices, with backend support dependent on each processing mode.
Platforms
Linux, Windows x64, and macOS packages or instructions are documented. The guide describes Apple Silicon acceleration separately from Intel packaging.

License, model weights & costs

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4] [5]

Hardware
The instructions document an NVIDIA T4 for inference and one to eight NVIDIA GPUs with at least 16 GB of GPU memory for training. The training feature cache is described as roughly 50 GB. System RAM and exact minimum inference disk are undocumented.
Software
Conda, PyTorch and CUDA 11.6 for the documented GPU path. Wine is mentioned only for the optional import/conversion route.
Platforms
The instructions describe an NVIDIA CUDA setup. A supported macOS or Windows inference path was not established in this source pass.

How it uses AI

How it uses AI

Cutroom indexes source material using local faster-whisper word timestamps and shot changes. An external agent can select edits and write an inspectable edit-decision list. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

Cutroom treats logging footage as a distinct stage and retains the evidence behind a proposed edit. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Planning a short, sourced excerpt from a long recording, with time references and a saved reversible edit. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The author provides a demonstration GIF and example logs with citations and cuts. These document the intended handoff without independently confirming every selected excerpt or render. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use the documented Python 3.12+ environment and FFmpeg. The local file route can start without online video access. Configure a model agent explicitly for editing decisions. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the documented Python 3.12+ environment and FFmpeg. The local file route can start without online video access. Configure a model agent explicitly for editing decisions.
uv sync
uv run cutroom --help
uv run cutroom log ./lecture.mp4 --whisper-model small

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Log a short local source so you can inspect its timestamps and shot boundaries.
  2. Have the tool show the cut plan and check each part against the original phrasing.
  3. Render a separate derivative from the saved decisions and verify framing and captions.

Beat This!

Documented creative workflow · MIT · first-profile

This is the first complete guide in the library.

Docs checked 2026-11-19 · 490 GitHub stars · Release v1.1.0 (2026-04-14) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Ready to try

The pinned offline beat-marker route has reproducible setup and independent integration evidence. Creative output has a documented evaluation. Results for the chosen source material remain unverified.

Scope: Beat This! 1.1.0, default final0 model, offline beat and downbeat markers from audio using a local CPU-supported PyTorch environment.

Evidence checked 2026-11-19; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code. Models, services and assets have separate terms. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The paper reports the authors’ benchmark, and a separate integration uses the model. Output quality for a creator’s music, meter changes and scene timing remains unverified. [4] [9]
  • Reproducible setup · verified: PyPI supplies version 1.1.0. The release and source identify the Python, PyTorch, audio decoder and CPU route. The tagged Musetric implementation invokes the same final0 File2Beats path on CPU when CUDA is unavailable. [5] [6] [1] [9] [10]
  • Maintenance and support · verified: The v1.1.0 release repaired checkpoint retrieval and clarified backend support. A later maintainer response resolved the storage outage, and today’s direct checkpoint metadata request returned HTTP 200. The check covers this released offline path. [6] [7] [11]
  • Independent use · verified: The released Musetric toolkit has a separate working integration that imports File2Beats and invokes the exact final0 checkpoint with CPU or CUDA selection. This is independent use of the scoped inference code. [9]
  • Models, services and costs · verified: The MIT software grant and maintainer’s separate MIT statement for released weights both apply. The official final0 endpoint responded publicly. Local CPU PyTorch and an audio decoder are sufficient for the scoped route. No paid inference service is required. Verify rights to source recordings separately. [1] [8] [11] [5]

This assessment is based on published sources. Hands-on verification remains pending. Offline timestamps do not imply low-latency live performance. The exact training and evaluation split matters when comparing output quality.

How it uses AI

A trained music model predicts beats and downbeats in audio. The package provides timestamps that a creator can map to timeline markers or visual events. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

The project supplies a standalone CLI, Python library and research materials for detecting beat positions in recorded music. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Deriving a draft beat grid from a cue for a projected rhythm study or music-led cut. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The researchers publish benchmark comparisons and a CLI example. The results are dataset-dependent. There is no established timing guarantee for an installation fed with an unexpected genre or live signal. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Install the PyPI package with a compatible PyTorch environment. The CLI can select CPU explicitly. FFmpeg or another supported audio decoder is needed for relevant formats. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Install the PyPI package with a compatible PyTorch environment. The CLI can select CPU explicitly. FFmpeg or another supported audio decoder is needed for relevant formats.
pip install beat-this
beat_this --help
beat_this path/to/audio.file -o path/to/output.beats

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Choose a small file with audible meter. Generate the beat and downbeat list.
  2. Listen against the reported markers and manually inspect meter changes or pickup notes.
  3. Import checked timestamps into a timeline or a separate cue script. Check end-to-end latency for a live version.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3] [4]

Hardware
CPU fallback is documented. The guide lists roughly 78 MB for a standard checkpoint and roughly 8.1 MB for the smaller one. Minimum system RAM and VRAM are undocumented.
Software
PyTorch 2.0+ and the listed Python dependencies. Non-WAV formats require a supported decoder. Optional DBN processing adds madmom and is not required by the default route.
Platforms
Python with supported PyTorch CPU or CUDA backend. A current OS-specific live-performance run was not established.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3] [4]

  • Weights: The authors explicitly release the project and published model weights under MIT. The training files, annotations, other source audio and optional DBN components retain their own terms.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. The authors explicitly release the project and published model weights under MIT. The training files, annotations, other source audio and optional DBN components retain their own terms.
  • Cost: Local compute and checkpoint download. Source audio rights are separate.

Why it merits attention

AIEV

Documented creative workflow · MIT · first-profile

This completes an earlier pending guide in the library. The current source review is dated 2026-11-19.

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Software licensing and a concrete creative AI use are supported. The remaining checks do not establish a recommendation.

Scope: Drafting captions with word highlighting for a Vietnamese video and correcting them in an editable production project.

Evidence checked 2026-11-19; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code. Models, services and assets have separate terms. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The repository documents the editor, automated checks and a rendering pipeline. Listening and spelling verification are still needed for clips with accents, speech overlap or names. [1] [3]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: Follow the maintainer’s platform-specific startup and dependency guide. Make sure FFmpeg, Node and browser dependencies are present and review which optional model providers are enabled. Independent reproduction has not been established in this review. [1] [3]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT applies to the source. Faster-whisper weights, hosted model endpoints and bundled footage, fonts and audio assets retain their own terms. Local editing uses compute and storage. Optional provider use may require an account or billing. [1] [3]

This assessment is based on published sources. Hands-on verification remains pending. Faster hardware encoding does not establish the speed of speech recognition. Online model and phone-upload integrations need their own privacy and network decisions.

How it uses AI

Faster-whisper produces timed words for Vietnamese karaoke captions. An external model partner can assist with editing decisions and review. FFmpeg performs ordinary rendering and measurements. [1] [3]

Introduction

AIEV combines a video project, editable captions and automated review measurements in one workspace for Vietnamese video. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Drafting captions with word highlighting for a Vietnamese video and correcting them in an editable production project. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The repository documents the editor, automated checks and a rendering pipeline. Listening and spelling verification are still needed for clips with accents, speech overlap or names. [1] [3]

Install

Follow the maintainer’s platform-specific startup and dependency guide. Make sure FFmpeg, Node and browser dependencies are present and review which optional model providers are enabled. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Follow the maintainer’s platform-specific startup and dependency guide. Make sure FFmpeg, Node and browser dependencies are present and review which optional model providers are enabled.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Add a small recording to a new project. Select a local transcription route and review the current captions.
  2. Fix missing diacritics, names and the highlighted timing before committing to the style.
  3. Render a separate copy and review sound, captions and text safe regions on the intended output.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
The guide describes NVIDIA CUDA and CPU int8 transcription, plus NVIDIA and Apple video encoders for rendering. General minimum memory and storage are not documented.
Software
Node.js and FFmpeg documented, with browser components where required. Local captions use Python and faster-whisper. External Claude Code or Gemini features are separate.
Platforms
Desktop documentation lists Windows, macOS and Linux with some platform-specific development and encoding steps. Not every optional model route is documented on every OS.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: MIT applies to the source. Faster-whisper weights, hosted model endpoints and bundled footage, fonts and audio assets retain their own terms.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT applies to the source. Faster-whisper weights, hosted model endpoints and bundled footage, fonts and audio assets retain their own terms.
  • Cost: Local editing uses compute and storage. Optional provider use may require an account or billing.

Why it merits attention

Included for the documented creative workflow. A complete guide and popularity do not establish output reliability. [1] [3]

Limitations

Faster hardware encoding does not establish the speed of speech recognition. Online model and phone-upload integrations need their own privacy and network decisions. [1] [3]

How it uses AI

Introduction

What it is good for

Scope: Building a rough depth reference from a cleared still for a composite or a supervised 2.5D layout.

  • Open-source software license · verified: The complete root software license was read and archived. The grant covers this project code. Models, services and assets have separate terms. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The authors publish depth comparisons with the paper and a model card. The work estimates relative geometry and leaves metric scale and occluded content unresolved. [1] [3] [4]

  • Maintenance and support · unknown: A release or active repository alone does not establish sustained support. Workflow-specific support and unresolved blockers need a deeper review.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use or a working third-party integration was not established for this selected workflow.

This assessment is based on published sources. Hands-on verification remains pending. Monocular depth does not establish scale or geometry hidden behind visible surfaces. Test the result in the intended compositor.

How it uses AI

The implementation reads features inside a Stable Diffusion 2.1-based pipeline and uses them for monocular depth estimation from one image. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

The project documents a Python inference route and published checkpoints for producing a depth layer from one still. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Building a rough depth reference from a cleared still for a composite or a supervised 2.5D layout. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The authors publish depth comparisons with the paper and a model card. The work estimates relative geometry and leaves metric scale and occluded content unresolved. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use the documented Conda Python 3.9 environment and install the listed inference dependencies. Point the local inference configuration at the reviewed compatible weights. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. Use the documented Conda Python 3.9 environment and install the listed inference dependencies. Point the local inference configuration at the reviewed compatible weights.
conda create -n PrimeDepth python=3.9
pip3 install -r requirements.txt

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Choose a scene with a clear subject and foreground/background boundaries.
  2. Generate a separate depth image using the cited project example and inspect thin edges and reflective areas.
  3. Use the result as an editing reference alongside the original image and refine the intended matte or parallax manually.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Accelerator requirements vary by documented backend. Minimum RAM, VRAM and disk for general use were not established.
Software
Python 3.11+. The source distinguishes CUDA PyTorch and Apple MLX engines. Optional diarization needs a separate model and authenticated vendor access.
Platforms
Native Linux with NVIDIA and Apple Silicon macOS are documented. Windows uses the WSL2 CUDA route with the stated device requirements. Native Windows is unsupported.

License, model weights & costs

  • Weights: MIT applies to VoxWeave. Qwen-family speech models, alignment weights and optional diarization have separate terms. Some optional weights are gated.
  • Commercial: Software use follows MIT. MIT applies to VoxWeave. Qwen-family speech models, alignment weights and optional diarization have separate terms. Some optional weights are gated.
  • Cost: Local compute and model downloads. Gated artifacts require their own access, which does not by itself imply a paid API.

Docker or Node 22 and pnpm 9, FFmpeg with libass/libwebp, Python and Pillow. OpenRouter and optional ElevenLabs accounts serve the scoped AI features.
Platforms
A container and browser client are documented. A native desktop installation or minimum host requirements for a full production remain unverified.

License, model weights & costs

  • Weights: Apache-2.0 applies to the code with inherited notices from its predecessor. Generation, narration and media assets have separate terms.
  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. Apache-2.0 applies to the code with inherited notices from its predecessor. Generation, narration and media assets have separate terms.
  • Cost: Rendering uses local or hosted compute. Official generation and optional narration use separately metered API services.

How it uses AI

Apple Vision estimates body, face and hand landmarks. The Swift sender streams them as OSC so a set of visual tools or another receiver can map movement to visuals and stage cues. [1] [3] [4]

Introduction

TrackOSC turns camera tracking into control messages for live media. A Mac or iPhone does the detection, and a separate renderer or interactive patch can respond to the signal. [1] [3] [4]

What it is good for

Driving an onstage typographic or particle effect from a phone or Mac camera with a locally inspectable signal. [1] [3] [4]

Demo & examples

The developer shows the sender and a Processing receiver with the same OSC schema and supplies synthetic-figure examples for optional visual receivers. [1] [3] [4]

Install

Use a signed release for the Apple sender and receiver, or the documented Apple development toolchain for source builds. Grant camera and local-network access for the stated workflow. [1] [3] [4]

  2. Use a signed release for the Apple sender and receiver, or the documented Apple development toolchain for source builds. Grant camera and local-network access for the stated workflow.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3] [4]

  1. Start the sender and receiver with a short camera check.
  2. Confirm OSC address, port and local-network accessibility before bringing another device into the setup.
  3. Map one stable landmark to one visual parameter and inspect the effect through a full range of motion.

Apple Vision on the sender. The source build uses Xcode 16+ with the iOS 18 and macOS 15 SDKs. Processing with oscP5 is one non-Apple receiver route.
Platforms
macOS 15+ and iOS 18+ for the documented senders. Cross-platform Processing, Python and browser receiver examples are separate from camera inference.

How it uses AI

MediaPipe Tasks estimates body and hand landmarks from a camera or NDI feed. A selectable OSC format broadcasts the resulting tracking signal to a stage or graphics application. [1] [3]

Introduction

Gesture gives a live patch separate hand and body pose signals from a camera. The preview and OSC activity display help a creator see which part of the chain is responding. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Connecting hand or body movement to a parameter in a live projection without writing the landmark detector. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The source documents a tracking preview with landmark overlays, message activity and receivers capable of using the same OSC stream. The latest observed release was v0.4.0 prerelease. [1] [3]

Install

The developer provides a signed and notarized Mac download for use on an Apple Silicon machine. Select the camera or NDI input and enter the receiver address in the app. [1] [3]

  1. Obtain the official source or release linked below.
  2. The developer provides a signed and notarized Mac download for use on an Apple Silicon machine. Select the camera or NDI input and enter the receiver address in the app.

First project

Start with a short, representative project and inspect the result before a larger batch. [1] [3]

  1. Connect a local camera first and confirm the preview detects landmarks.
  2. Select an OSC message format your receiver can read and confirm the port before running the scene.
  3. Keep the gesture and receiver response simple for the first test.

Hardware & software

Requirements below are documented for this workflow. Unspecified limits are not inferred. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum system RAM, VRAM and total disk space are not documented in the reviewed instructions.
Software
MediaPipe Tasks, an OSC-capable receiver, and optional NDI input. Camera is the base scope.
Platforms
Apple Silicon Mac running macOS 13+ is the documented sender. Receiver platform depends on the chosen OSC tool.

The full software license was reviewed. Model weights, assets and services remain separate. [2] [1] [3]

  • Commercial: Software use follows Apache-2.0. The Apache-2.0 app grant applies to the project source. MediaPipe models and optional NDI components are governed separately.
  • Cost: Local camera and processing in the scoped route. Receiving applications may have distinct licenses and costs.
